Um… I guess what finally made me come in is work. I’m an accountant, and I’ll be sitting there with an audit workpaper open, and I just… stare at it. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.

It’s strange because I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. I mean, I’ve always been the person people ask to check formulas or tie things out, and now I’m just sitting there, and then I notice the little cursor blinking, and somehow that makes it worse. Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I open a spreadsheet or my email, I hear a clear voice like it’s coming from just behind my laptop saying, “You missed it, they know,” even though no one else is in the room.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already lost before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to plan hikes, take photos, cook on Sundays, and volunteer at the library, but now those things just feel like items on a checklist I don’t care enough to start.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Food has mostly become something I force down because I know I should, and my work skirts have gotten loose even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, my body feels like I’ve been up closing an audit for three days, and simple things like showering or sorting insurance paperwork can wipe me out.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: I never heard that voice or believed the firm was secretly building a case against me before this depression settled in; those experiences only started after my mood and energy had been low for a while.\n- key: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Two weeks sounds very specific, and I’m bad at remembering things in neat blocks like that.

There were times I felt off or overwhelmed, but whether it was most of the day, every day, and for two weeks… I can’t really tell. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of it that way, but I still can’t really pin one down. Like, I can remember being low or kind of checked out at different points, but “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks”… I don’t know.

My memory doesn’t sort things like that. I remember little scenes, like walking to work with the same coffee every morning and realizing I’d taken three sips and it was already cold, or leaving laundry in the dryer so long it got wrinkled and then just running it again instead of folding it. But whether that was two straight weeks of feeling down, I can’t say for sure.

So… I guess I don’t really know. I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. I’m trying to line up my life in my head like, college, first job, moving apartments, that year my upstairs neighbor had those wind chimes that kept me awake—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. It’s all kind of blurry when I try to separate it out that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any other times in your life, besides this current period, when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know I keep sort of circling this, but no, I can’t really think of another time that clearly fits that. I mean, I’ve had periods where I wasn’t myself, or I was dragging through things, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” my brain just kind of… loses the thread.

Like I can remember little things from different years, the office carpet in my first job, or this one winter where I kept buying the same soup for lunch and then getting sick of it, but I don’t know if I was actually down every day or just stressed or tired. I mean, I’ve had periods where I wasn’t myself, or I was dragging through things, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” my brain just kind of… loses the thread.\n\nLike I can remember little things from different years, the office carpet in my first job, or this one winter where I kept buying the same soup for lunch and then getting sick of it, but I don’t know if I was actually down every day or just stressed or tired. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I don’t have another definite time I can point to.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any time in your life when you had a period of feeling down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks, and then fully recovered for a while—like at least a couple of months—before another period like that happened?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It’s not even always like crying or something obvious. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the whole day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll just lie there thinking about stupid things, like whether I forgot to run the dishwasher or if the neighbor’s dog is barking again—sorry, that’s not really the point. It just feels like everything starts out already… empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or like you have no energy, even if you haven't done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. But yes, I feel tired even when I haven’t really done anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Food just feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I want. But yeah… around then. Since then it’s just been there most mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough at things, or blaming yourself for things that go wrong, even when it's not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I’ve always been the person people ask to check formulas or tie things out, and now I’m just sitting there, and then I notice the little cursor blinking, and somehow that makes it worse. Anyway… people at work are starting to notice I’m not keeping up, so I thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… down, I guess. It’s not even always like crying or something obvious. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the whole day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll just lie there thinking about stupid things, like whether I forgot to run the dishwasher or if the neighbor’s dog is barking again—sorry, that’s not really the point. It just feels like everything starts out already… empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or like you have no energy, even if you haven't done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s kind of ridiculous, actually, because I can be in bed the whole night, and then when I get up, my body feels like I’ve been awake for days, like I just finished one of those horrible audit close weeks.\n\nEven small things wear me out. Like showering—just showering—can feel like this whole project. Or I’ll look at insurance paperwork on the counter, and I know it’s just sorting forms, but after a few minutes I feel completely drained. And then I start thinking I should really reorganize that drawer where all the old mail is, which… I don’t know why I’m bringing that up. But yes, I feel tired even when I haven’t really done anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Food just feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I want. I’ll stand in the kitchen and open the fridge and then get distracted by, like, whether the leftovers container has the right lid on it, and then I just close it again.\n\nI do make myself eat sometimes because I know I should, but it’s more like forcing it down. And my work skirts have been getting loose lately, which is annoying because I only have a couple that fit right… or used to fit right, I guess. I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness in the morning, the tiredness, and losing interest in things?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about seven months now. It kind of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing thing.\n\nAt first I thought I was just stressed because, you know, budgets and bills and trying to make the grocery list stretch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah… around then. Since then it’s just been there most mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough at things, or blaming yourself for things that go wrong, even when it's not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, but I’m not sure if it’s like… “not good enough” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay things I said at work and think I sounded stupid, but then I also do that thing where I remember I forgot to buy dish soap, and then I’m thinking about the whole sink area needing to be cleaned, and it spirals into this list of stuff. Sorry, that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nI guess I blame myself for some things, but I can’t tell if it’s more than anyone else would. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before — like you’re stuck in a fog and can’t get motivated — or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had times where I didn’t want to do things, like in college during finals I’d just sit there staring at my notes, and then my roommate would be making ramen at midnight and the whole room smelled like those little seasoning packets… sorry.\n\nThis feels different, maybe, but I don’t know if it’s the first time or if I’m just noticing it more now. I can’t really sort it out cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning the way you normally do?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly not sure. I can think of times where I was really overwhelmed, like when I first started working and everything felt impossible for a while, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nI mean, I still got up and went in, mostly because there were deadlines and my supervisor had this way of emailing with just “please advise” that made my stomach drop—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly that there was another period like this before. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning at work or home for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Two weeks sounds very specific, and I’m bad at remembering things in neat blocks like that.\n\nThere were times I felt off or overwhelmed, but whether it was most of the day, every day, and for two weeks… I can’t really tell. I remember more random details, like sitting at my kitchen table with mail piled up and those little credit card offers everywhere, and thinking I should shred them but then not doing it. But I don’t know if that means I was depressed or just… avoiding chores.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I can’t clearly point to a time before now and say yes, that was the same thing for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to any time before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of it that way, but I still can’t really pin one down. Like, I can remember being low or kind of checked out at different points, but “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks”… I don’t know.\n\nMy memory doesn’t sort things like that. I remember little scenes, like walking to work with the same coffee every morning and realizing I’d taken three sips and it was already cold, or leaving laundry in the dryer so long it got wrinkled and then just running it again instead of folding it. But whether that was two straight weeks of feeling down, I can’t say for sure.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. And then I start thinking I should really reorganize that drawer where all the old mail is, which… I don’t know why I’m bringing that up. But yes, I feel tired even when I haven’t really done anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Food just feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I want. I’ll stand in the kitchen and open the fridge and then get distracted by, like, whether the leftovers container has the right lid on it, and then I just close it again.\n\nI do make myself eat sometimes because I know I should, but it’s more like forcing it down. And my work skirts have been getting loose lately, which is annoying because I only have a couple that fit right… or used to fit right, I guess. I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness in the morning, the tiredness, and losing interest in things?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about seven months now. It kind of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing thing.\n\nAt first I thought I was just stressed because, you know, budgets and bills and trying to make the grocery list stretch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah… around then. Since then it’s just been there most mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough at things, or blaming yourself for things that go wrong, even when it's not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, but I’m not sure if it’s like… “not good enough” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay things I said at work and think I sounded stupid, but then I also do that thing where I remember I forgot to buy dish soap, and then I’m thinking about the whole sink area needing to be cleaned, and it spirals into this list of stuff. Sorry, that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nI guess I blame myself for some things, but I can’t tell if it’s more than anyone else would. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before — like you’re stuck in a fog and can’t get motivated — or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had times where I didn’t want to do things, like in college during finals I’d just sit there staring at my notes, and then my roommate would be making ramen at midnight and the whole room smelled like those little seasoning packets… sorry.\n\nThis feels different, maybe, but I don’t know if it’s the first time or if I’m just noticing it more now. I can’t really sort it out cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning the way you normally do?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly not sure. I can think of times where I was really overwhelmed, like when I first started working and everything felt impossible for a while, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nI mean, I still got up and went in, mostly because there were deadlines and my supervisor had this way of emailing with just “please advise” that made my stomach drop—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly that there was another period like this before. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning at work or home for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Two weeks sounds very specific, and I’m bad at remembering things in neat blocks like that.\n\nThere were times I felt off or overwhelmed, but whether it was most of the day, every day, and for two weeks… I can’t really tell. I remember more random details, like sitting at my kitchen table with mail piled up and those little credit card offers everywhere, and thinking I should shred them but then not doing it. But I don’t know if that means I was depressed or just… avoiding chores.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I can’t clearly point to a time before now and say yes, that was the same thing for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to any time before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of it that way, but I still can’t really pin one down. Like, I can remember being low or kind of checked out at different points, but “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks”… I don’t know.\n\nMy memory doesn’t sort things like that. I remember little scenes, like walking to work with the same coffee every morning and realizing I’d taken three sips and it was already cold, or leaving laundry in the dryer so long it got wrinkled and then just running it again instead of folding it. But whether that was two straight weeks of feeling down, I can’t say for sure.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. I’m trying to line up my life in my head like, college, first job, moving apartments, that year my upstairs neighbor had those wind chimes that kept me awake—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’ve had rough patches, but I can’t clearly identify even one definite two-week stretch like that before now, let alone more than one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can answer that with any real confidence. I keep circling around it, like maybe there were times I felt low, and then times I was functioning better, but I don’t know if that counts as fully recovered.

It’s kind of like when I’m doing reconciliations at work and one number is off, and you keep checking the same column over and over even though the problem might be somewhere else entirely. That’s how my memory feels with this. I can remember being busy, tired, stressed… but not clearly enough to say there was one two-week period, then a couple months of being completely okay, then another one.

So, no, I don’t have a clear example of that.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess what finally made me come in is work. I’m an accountant, and I’ll be sitting there with an audit workpaper open, and I just… stare at it. Like twenty minutes can go by before I even move my hand to type anything.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. I mean, I’ve always been the person people ask to check formulas or tie things out, and now I’m just sitting there, and then I notice the little cursor blinking, and somehow that makes it worse. Anyway… people at work are starting to notice I’m not keeping up, so I thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… down, I guess. It’s not even always like crying or something obvious. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the whole day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll just lie there thinking about stupid things, like whether I forgot to run the dishwasher or if the neighbor’s dog is barking again—sorry, that’s not really the point. It just feels like everything starts out already… empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or like you have no energy, even if you haven't done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s kind of ridiculous, actually, because I can be in bed the whole night, and then when I get up, my body feels like I’ve been awake for days, like I just finished one of those horrible audit close weeks.\n\nEven small things wear me out. Like showering—just showering—can feel like this whole project. Or I’ll look at insurance paperwork on the counter, and I know it’s just sorting forms, but after a few minutes I feel completely drained. And then I start thinking I should really reorganize that drawer where all the old mail is, which… I don’t know why I’m bringing that up. But yes, I feel tired even when I haven’t really done anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Food just feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I want. I’ll stand in the kitchen and open the fridge and then get distracted by, like, whether the leftovers container has the right lid on it, and then I just close it again.\n\nI do make myself eat sometimes because I know I should, but it’s more like forcing it down. And my work skirts have been getting loose lately, which is annoying because I only have a couple that fit right… or used to fit right, I guess. I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness in the morning, the tiredness, and losing interest in things?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about seven months now. It kind of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing thing.\n\nAt first I thought I was just stressed because, you know, budgets and bills and trying to make the grocery list stretch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah… around then. Since then it’s just been there most mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough at things, or blaming yourself for things that go wrong, even when it's not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, but I’m not sure if it’s like… “not good enough” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay things I said at work and think I sounded stupid, but then I also do that thing where I remember I forgot to buy dish soap, and then I’m thinking about the whole sink area needing to be cleaned, and it spirals into this list of stuff. Sorry, that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nI guess I blame myself for some things, but I can’t tell if it’s more than anyone else would. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before — like you’re stuck in a fog and can’t get motivated — or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had times where I didn’t want to do things, like in college during finals I’d just sit there staring at my notes, and then my roommate would be making ramen at midnight and the whole room smelled like those little seasoning packets… sorry.\n\nThis feels different, maybe, but I don’t know if it’s the first time or if I’m just noticing it more now. I can’t really sort it out cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning the way you normally do?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly not sure. I can think of times where I was really overwhelmed, like when I first started working and everything felt impossible for a while, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nI mean, I still got up and went in, mostly because there were deadlines and my supervisor had this way of emailing with just “please advise” that made my stomach drop—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly that there was another period like this before. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning at work or home for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Two weeks sounds very specific, and I’m bad at remembering things in neat blocks like that.\n\nThere were times I felt off or overwhelmed, but whether it was most of the day, every day, and for two weeks… I can’t really tell. I remember more random details, like sitting at my kitchen table with mail piled up and those little credit card offers everywhere, and thinking I should shred them but then not doing it. But I don’t know if that means I was depressed or just… avoiding chores.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I can’t clearly point to a time before now and say yes, that was the same thing for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to any time before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of it that way, but I still can’t really pin one down. Like, I can remember being low or kind of checked out at different points, but “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks”… I don’t know.\n\nMy memory doesn’t sort things like that. I remember little scenes, like walking to work with the same coffee every morning and realizing I’d taken three sips and it was already cold, or leaving laundry in the dryer so long it got wrinkled and then just running it again instead of folding it. But whether that was two straight weeks of feeling down, I can’t say for sure.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. I’m trying to line up my life in my head like, college, first job, moving apartments, that year my upstairs neighbor had those wind chimes that kept me awake—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’ve had rough patches, but I can’t clearly identify even one definite two-week stretch like that before now, let alone more than one. It’s all kind of blurry when I try to separate it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other times in your life, besides this current period, when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I know I keep sort of circling this, but no, I can’t really think of another time that clearly fits that. I mean, I’ve had periods where I wasn’t myself, or I was dragging through things, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” my brain just kind of… loses the thread.\n\nLike I can remember little things from different years, the office carpet in my first job, or this one winter where I kept buying the same soup for lunch and then getting sick of it, but I don’t know if I was actually down every day or just stressed or tired. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I don’t have another definite time I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in your life when you had a period of feeling down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks, and then fully recovered for a while—like at least a couple of months—before another period like that happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can map it out that way. Like… down for two weeks, then completely okay for months, then down again—I can’t point to that.\n\nI can remember phases where life felt flatter, or harder to get through, but I don’t have this clear before-and-after thing in my head. I’m thinking of old calendars, actually, because I used to color-code my work deadlines in this very intense way, and sometimes whole months were just covered in blue highlighter… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nWhat I mean is, no, I don’t know of a clear time where that happened and then I fully recovered for a couple months before it happened again.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of at least two separate times in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m sorry, I know I’m probably sounding like a broken record. I just… I don’t have a clear memory that fits that exact pattern.

I keep thinking maybe if I looked through old calendars or work emails I could piece it together, because I remember things more by deadlines than by how I felt. Like quarterly close, audits, birthdays I forgot to RSVP to—those stick more than the actual timeline of my mood.

But sitting here, no, I can’t honestly say there was a definite two-week period like that, then a couple months where I was fully back to normal, and then another one. I don’t want to give you a made-up answer just because I feel like I should have one.

I’m an accountant, and I’ll be sitting there with an audit workpaper open, and I just… stare at it. I mean, I’ve always been the person people ask to check formulas or tie things out, and now I’m just sitting there, and then I notice the little cursor blinking, and somehow that makes it worse. Anyway… people at work are starting to notice I’m not keeping up, so I thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… down, I guess. It’s not even always like crying or something obvious. Most days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the whole day is already ruined before I’ve even gotten out of bed.\n\nAnd then I’ll just lie there thinking about stupid things, like whether I forgot to run the dishwasher or if the neighbor’s dog is barking again—sorry, that’s not really the point. It just feels like everything starts out already… empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or like you have no energy, even if you haven't done anything physically or mentally taxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s kind of ridiculous, actually, because I can be in bed the whole night, and then when I get up, my body feels like I’ve been awake for days, like I just finished one of those horrible audit close weeks.\n\nEven small things wear me out. Like showering—just showering—can feel like this whole project. Or I’ll look at insurance paperwork on the counter, and I know it’s just sorting forms, but after a few minutes I feel completely drained. And then I start thinking I should really reorganize that drawer where all the old mail is, which… I don’t know why I’m bringing that up. But yes, I feel tired even when I haven’t really done anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Food just feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I want. I’ll stand in the kitchen and open the fridge and then get distracted by, like, whether the leftovers container has the right lid on it, and then I just close it again.\n\nI do make myself eat sometimes because I know I should, but it’s more like forcing it down. And my work skirts have been getting loose lately, which is annoying because I only have a couple that fit right… or used to fit right, I guess. I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness in the morning, the tiredness, and losing interest in things?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about seven months now. It kind of started after my husband stopped working, when his company did that downsizing thing.\n\nAt first I thought I was just stressed because, you know, budgets and bills and trying to make the grocery list stretch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah… around then. Since then it’s just been there most mornings.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough at things, or blaming yourself for things that go wrong, even when it's not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, but I’m not sure if it’s like… “not good enough” exactly.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay things I said at work and think I sounded stupid, but then I also do that thing where I remember I forgot to buy dish soap, and then I’m thinking about the whole sink area needing to be cleaned, and it spirals into this list of stuff. Sorry, that’s probably not what you meant.\n\nI guess I blame myself for some things, but I can’t tell if it’s more than anyone else would. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before — like you’re stuck in a fog and can’t get motivated — or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had times where I didn’t want to do things, like in college during finals I’d just sit there staring at my notes, and then my roommate would be making ramen at midnight and the whole room smelled like those little seasoning packets… sorry.\n\nThis feels different, maybe, but I don’t know if it’s the first time or if I’m just noticing it more now. I can’t really sort it out cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning the way you normally do?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly not sure. I can think of times where I was really overwhelmed, like when I first started working and everything felt impossible for a while, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing.\n\nI mean, I still got up and went in, mostly because there were deadlines and my supervisor had this way of emailing with just “please advise” that made my stomach drop—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly that there was another period like this before. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, lost interest in things, or had trouble functioning at work or home for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Two weeks sounds very specific, and I’m bad at remembering things in neat blocks like that.\n\nThere were times I felt off or overwhelmed, but whether it was most of the day, every day, and for two weeks… I can’t really tell. I remember more random details, like sitting at my kitchen table with mail piled up and those little credit card offers everywhere, and thinking I should shred them but then not doing it. But I don’t know if that means I was depressed or just… avoiding chores.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I can’t clearly point to a time before now and say yes, that was the same thing for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to any time before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of it that way, but I still can’t really pin one down. Like, I can remember being low or kind of checked out at different points, but “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks”… I don’t know.\n\nMy memory doesn’t sort things like that. I remember little scenes, like walking to work with the same coffee every morning and realizing I’d taken three sips and it was already cold, or leaving laundry in the dryer so long it got wrinkled and then just running it again instead of folding it. But whether that was two straight weeks of feeling down, I can’t say for sure.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t really know. I can’t clearly remember a time before this that fits that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. I’m trying to line up my life in my head like, college, first job, moving apartments, that year my upstairs neighbor had those wind chimes that kept me awake—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’ve had rough patches, but I can’t clearly identify even one definite two-week stretch like that before now, let alone more than one. It’s all kind of blurry when I try to separate it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other times in your life, besides this current period, when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I know I keep sort of circling this, but no, I can’t really think of another time that clearly fits that. I mean, I’ve had periods where I wasn’t myself, or I was dragging through things, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks,” my brain just kind of… loses the thread.\n\nLike I can remember little things from different years, the office carpet in my first job, or this one winter where I kept buying the same soup for lunch and then getting sick of it, but I don’t know if I was actually down every day or just stressed or tired. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I don’t have another definite time I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in your life when you had a period of feeling down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks, and then fully recovered for a while—like at least a couple of months—before another period like that happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can map it out that way. Like… down for two weeks, then completely okay for months, then down again—I can’t point to that.\n\nI can remember phases where life felt flatter, or harder to get through, but I don’t have this clear before-and-after thing in my head. I’m thinking of old calendars, actually, because I used to color-code my work deadlines in this very intense way, and sometimes whole months were just covered in blue highlighter… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nWhat I mean is, no, I don’t know of a clear time where that happened and then I fully recovered for a couple months before it happened again.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of at least two separate times in your life before this current period when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I can think of times that were bad or foggy, but not clearly “most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks.”\n\nIt’s weird because I remember random details, like what bus I took to work or which mug I kept at my desk, but I can’t line up my mood neatly enough to say, yes, that was one episode and that was another.
